But the Prophets easily reconcile these apparent contrarieties; for they say, that for a time there would be no kingdom, or at least that it would be disturbed by many calamities, so that there would appear no outward form of a kingdom, and no visible glory. As then they say this, and at the same time add, that there would come a restoration, that God would establish this kingdom by the power of his Christ, — as then the Prophets say this, they show that its perpetuity would really appear and be exhibited in Christ. Though then the kingdom had for some time fallen, this does not militate against the other predictions. This then is the right view of the subject: for Christ at length appeared, on whose head rests the true diadem or crown, and who has been elected by Gods and is the legitimate king, and who, having risen from the dead, reigns and now sits at the Father’s right hand, and his throne shall not fail to the end of the world; nay, the world shall be renovated, and Christ’s kingdom shall continue, though in another form, after the resurrection, as Paul shows to us; and yet Christ shall be really a king for ever.

[BODY] And the Prophet, by saying, <sty c="800000" i="1">as in ancient days,</sty><sty c="000000" i="1"> </sty>confirms this truth, that the dignity of the kingdom would not continue uniform, but that the restoration would yet be such as to make it clearly evident that God had not in vain promised an eternal kingdom to David. Flourish then shall the kingdom of David for ever. But this has not been the case; for when the people returned from exile, Zerobabel, it is true, and also many others,

--- PAGE 253 ---

[BODY] obtained kingly power; yet what was it but precarious? They became even tributaries to the kings of the Persian and of the Medes. It then follows, that the kingdom of Israel never flourished, nor had there existed among the people anything but a limited power; we must, therefore, necessarily come to Christ and his kingdom. We hence see that the words of the Prophet cannot be otherwise understood than of Christ. It follows —

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300912></sty>AMOS 9:12

[BODY] 12. That they may possess the remnant of Edom, and of all the heathen, which are called by my name, saith the LORD that doeth this.<sty c="800000" i="0"> F44</sty>
[BODY] By these words the Prophet shows that the kingdom under Christ would be more renowned and larger than it had ever been under David. Since then the kingdom had been greatest in dignity, and wealth, and power, in the age of David, the Prophet here says, that its borders would be enlarged; for then he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">Possess shall the Israelites the remnant of Edom</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty> He speaks here in common of the Israelites and of the Jews, as before, at the beginning of the last chapter, he threatened both. But we now apprehend what he means, — that Edom shall come under the yoke.

[BODY] And it is sufficiently evident why he mentions here especially the Idumeans, and that is because they had been most inveterate enemies; and vicinity gave them greater opportunity for doing harm. As then the Idumeans harassed the miserable Jews, and gave them no respite, this is the reason why the Prophet says that they would come under the power of his elect people. He afterwards adds, that all nations would come also to the Jews. He speaks first of the Idumeans, but he also adds all other nations. I cannot finish today.

[CENTERED] PRAYER.

[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">Grant, Almighty God, that as we see everywhere so many evident</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">tokens of thy displeasure, and more grievous ones are impeding, if</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">we indeed duly consider how grievously we have provoked thy</sty>

--- PAGE 254 ---

[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">wrath, and how wickedly also the whole world at this day rages</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">against thee and at the same time abuses thy many and excellent</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">benefits, — O grant, that we may ever remember thy covenant and</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">entertain a perpetual confidence in thy only-begotten Son, that</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">whenever it may please thee to sift us, thou mayest keep us in</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">safety, until we come, not into any earthly storehouse but into thy</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">celestial kingdom, where we may become partakers of that glory</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">which thy Son has obtained for us, who has once for all redeemed</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">us that we may ever remain under his guardianship and protection.</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">Amen.</sty>

--- PAGE 255 ---

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">LECTURE SIXTY-NINTH</sty>

[BODY] In yesterday’s Lecture, we could not finish the verse in which Amos says, that the Idumeans and other nations would come under the power of the people of God. As to the first clause there is no ambiguity, but the latter admits of two meanings. Some take its sense to be this, “Other nations on whom my name is called:” and others refer this to the children of Abraham in this way, “That possess the remnants of Edom and all nations they may, upon whom,” etc.; that is, that they on whom my name is called, even the descendants of Abraham, may possess the Idumeans and all other nations. If we choose the reference to be made to the chosen people, the order of the words seems to be somewhat broken; and yet this sense is very suitable, — that possess their enemies the faithful may, on whom my name is called; for the reason appears to be here expressed by the Prophet, why he promised a large kingdom to the Israelites, and that is, because they were enrolled in God’s name, the Lord owned them as his people, inasmuch as he had chosen and adopted them in the person of their father Abraham. But if the other view be more approved, then the particle <sty c="008080" i="0">אשר</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">asher,</sty> is not, as I think, a pronoun relative, but an adverb expressing a cause, “That they may possess the remnants of Edom and all nations, for my name shall have been, or shall be, called on them:”<sty c="800000" i="0"> F45</sty> for who can have possession of this right or title but those who, having been aliens, shall pass over into the family of Abraham? Israel is indeed said to possess whatever comes from another quarter, and is incorporated into the body of the Church.

[BODY] But on this point I will not contend; for this main thing is evident to us, — that the extension of the kingdom under Christ is here promised as though he had said that the Jews were included within narrow bounds, even when the kingdom of David especially flourished, but that God would under Christ extend their borders, and cause them to rule far and wide. What it is to call God’s name on a people, we have elsewhere stated. Let us now go on with the context.

--- PAGE 256 ---

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300913></sty>AMOS 9:13

[BODY] 13. Behold, the days come, saith the LORD, that the plowman shall overtake the reaper, and the treader of grapes him that soweth seed; and the mountains shall drop sweet wine, and all the hills shall melt.
[BODY] Here the Prophet describes the felicity which shall be under the reign of Christ: and we know that whenever the Prophets set forth promises of a happy and prosperous state to God’s people, they adopt metaphorical expressions, and say, that abundance of all good things shall flow, that there shall be the most fruitful produce, that provisions shall be bountifully supplied; for they accommodated their mode of speaking to the notions of that ancient people; it is therefore no wonders if they sometimes speak to them as to children. At the same time, the Spirit under these figurative expressions declares, that the kingdom of Christ shall in every way be happy and blessed, or that the Church of God, which means the same thing, shall be blessed, when Christ shall begin to reign.

[BODY] Hence he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">Coming are the days, saith Jehovah, and the plowman shall</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">draw nigh</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> or meet,<sty c="000000" i="1"> </sty><sty c="800000" i="1">the reaper</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty> The Prophet no doubt refers to the blessing mentioned by Moses in <sty c="800000" i="0"><032601></sty>Leviticus 26:1 for the Prophets borrowed thence their mode of speaking, to add more credit and authority to what they taught. And Moses uses nearly the same words, — that the vintage shall meet the harvest, and also that sowing shall meet the plowing: and this is the case, when God supplies abundance of corn and wine, and when the season is pleasant and favorable. We then see what the Prophet means, that is, that God would so bless his people, that he would suffer no lack of good things.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">The plowman then shall come nigh the reaper; and the treader of grapes,</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">the bearer of seed.</sty> When they shall finish the harvest, they shall begin to plow, for the season will be most favorable; and then when they shall complete their vintage, they shall sow. Thus the fruitfulness, as I have said, of all produce is mentioned.

--- PAGE 257 ---

[BODY] The Prophet now speaks in a hyperbolical language, and says<sty c="000000" i="1">, </sty><sty c="800000" i="1">Mountains</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">shall drop sweetness, and all the hills shall melt</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">, </sty>that is, milk shall flow down. We indeed know that this has never happened; but this manner of speaking is common and often occurs in Scripture. The sum of the whole is, that there will be no common or ordinary abundance of blessings, but what will exceed belief, and even the course of nature, as the very mountains shall as it were flow down. It now follows —

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300914></sty>AMOS 9:14

[BODY] 14. And I will bring again the captivity of my people of Israel, and they shall build the waste cities, and inhabit them; and they shall plant vineyards, and drink the wine thereof; they shall also make gardens, and eat the fruit of them.
[BODY] As the prophecy we have noticed was one difficult to be believed, especially when the people were led away into exile, the Prophet comes to the help of this lack of faith, and shows that this would be no hindrance to God to lead his people to the felicity of which he speaks. These things seem indeed to be quite contrary, the one to the other, — that the people, spoiled of all dignity, should be driven to a far country to live in miserable exile, and that they should also be scattered into various parts and oppressed by base tyranny; — and that at the same time a most flourishing condition should be promised them, and that such an extension of their kingdom should be promised them, as had never been previously witnessed. Lest then their present calamities should fill their minds with fear and bind them fast in despair; he says that the Israelites shall return from exile, not indeed all; but as we have already seen, this promise is addressed to the elect alone: at the same time he speaks here simply of the people. But, this prophecy is connected with other prophecies: it ought not therefore to be extended except to that remnant seed, of whom we have before taken notice.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Restore</sty> then <sty c="800000" i="1">will I the captivity of my people Israel</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">;</sty> and then, <sty c="800000" i="1">They shall</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">build nested cities and dwell there; they shall plant vineyards, and their</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">wine shall they drink; they shall make gardens, and shall eat their fruit.</sty> He

--- PAGE 258 ---

[BODY] reminds the people here of the blessings mentioned in the Law. They must indeed have known that the hand of the Lord was opposed to them in their exile. Hence the Prophet now shows, that as soon as the Lord would again begin to be propitious to them, there would be a new state of things; for when God shows his smiling countenance, prosperity follows and a blessed success in all things. This then is what the Prophet now intends to show, that the miserable exiles might not faint in despair, when the Lord chastised them. It follows at last —

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300915></sty>AMOS 9:15

[BODY] 15. And I will plant them upon their land, and they shall no more be pulled up out of their land which I have given them, saith the LORD thy God.
[BODY] The Prophet further mentions here a quiet dwellings in the land, for it was not enough for the people to be restored to their country, except they lived there in safety and quietness; for they might soon afterwards have been removed again. It would have been better for them to pine away in exile, than to be restored for the sake, as it were, of sporting with them, and in a short time to be again conquered by their enemies, and to be led away into another country. Therefore the Prophet says, that the people, when restored, would be in a state of tranquillity.

[BODY] And he uses a most suitable comparison, when he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">I will plant them in</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">their own land, nor shall they be pulled up any more</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">:</sty> for how can we have a settled place to dwell in, except the Lord locates us somewhere? We are indeed as it were flitting beings on the earth, and we may at any moment be tossed here and there as the chaff. We have therefore no settled dwelling, except as far as we are planted by the hand of God, or as far as God assigns to us a certain habitation, and is pleased to make us rest in quietness. This is what the Prophet means by saying, <sty c="800000" i="1">I will plant them in</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">their own land, nor shall they any more be pulled up</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">. </sty>How so? “Because, he says, I have given to them the land”. He had indeed given it to them before, but he suffered them to be pulled up when they had polluted the land. But now God declares that his grace would outweigh the sins of the

--- PAGE 259 ---

[BODY] people; as though he said, “However unworthy the people are, who dwell in this land, my gift will yet be effectual: for I will not regard what they deserve at my hands, but as I have given them this land, they shall obtain it.” We now apprehend the meaning of the Prophet.

[BODY] Now, if we look on what afterwards happened, it may appear that this prophecy has never been fulfilled. The Jews indeed returned to their own country, but it was only a small number: and besides, it was so far from being the case, that they ruled over neighboring nations, that they became on the contrary tributaries to them: and further still, the limits of their rule were ever narrow, even when they were able to shake off the yoke. In what sense then has God promised what we have just explained? We see this when we come to Christ; for it will then be evident that nothing has been in vain foretold: though the Jews have not ruled as to the outward appearance, yet the kingdom of God was then propagated among all nations, from the rising to the setting of the sun; and then, as we have said in other places, the Jews reigned.

[BODY] Further, what is here said of the abundance of corn and wine, must be explained with reference to the nature of Christ’s kingdom. As then the kingdom of Christ is spiritual, it is enough for us, that it abounds in spiritual blessings: and the Jews, whom God reserved for himself as a remnant, were satisfied with this spiritual abundance.

[BODY] If any one objects and says, that the Prophet does not speak here allegorically; the answer is ready at hand, even this, — that it is a manner of speaking everywhere found in Scripture, that a happy state is painted as it were before our eyes, by setting before us the conveniences of the present life and earthly blessings: this may especially be observed in the Prophets, for they accommodated their style, as we have already stated, to the capacities of a rude and weak people. But as this subject has been discussed elsewhere more at large, I only touch on it now as in passing and lightly. Now follows the Prophecy of Obadiah, who is commonly called Abdiah.<sty c="800000" i="0"> F46</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">END OF THE COMMENTARIES ON AMOS.</sty>

--- PAGE 260 ---

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 2</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">1</sty> Thus saith Jehovah,— For three transgressions of Moab, and for four, I will not be propitious to him; For he burnt the bones of the king of Edom into ashes:

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">2</sty> And I will send a fire on Moab, And it shall devour the palaces of Kirioth; And die in a tumult shall Moab, With shouting, at the sound of the trumpet;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">4</sty> Thus saith Jehovah,— For three transgressions of Judah, and for four, I will not be propitious to him; For they have despised the law of Jehovah, And his statutes have they not kept; And to go astray have their lies made them, After which their fathers walked:

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">5</sty> And I will send a fire on Judah, And it shall devour the palaces of Jerusalem.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">6</sty> Thus saith Jehovah,— For three transgressions of Israel, and for four, I will not be propitious to him; For they have sold the just for silver, and the poor for shoes;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">7</sty> Panting for the dust of the earth on the head of the needy, They turn aside the way of the miserable; The son and his father enter in into the <sty c="000000" i="1">same</sty> maid, So that they profane my holy name;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">8</sty> And on pledged garments they lie near every altar, And the wine of the condemned they drink In the house of their god.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">9</sty> Yet I exterminated the Amorite before their face, Whose tallness was like the tallness of cedars, And who was strong like the oak; And I destroyed his fruit above and his root beneath;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">10</sty> And I brought you up from the land of Egypt, And led you in the desert forty years, That you might possess the land of the Amorite;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">11</sty> And I raised up Prophets from your sons, And from your youths, Nazarites;— Have I not done all this, O children of Israel, saith Jehovah:

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">13</sty> Behold, I am fast bound under you, (196) As a wagon is bound, which is full of sheaves.—

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">14</sty> Perish shall flight from the swift, And the valiant shall not confirm his strength, And the strong shall not deliver his life,

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">15</sty> And the holder of the bow shall not stand, And the swift on foot shall not escape, Nor shall he who mounts a horse save his life;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">16</sty> Yea, the strong of heart, among the valiant, Shall flee away naked in that day, saith Jehovah.

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 3</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">1</sty> Hear ye this word which Jehovah pronounces Against you, children of Israel—against the whole family, Which I brought up from the land of Egypt, saying,—

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">2</sty> “Only you have I known of all the families of the earth, “I will therefore visit on you all your iniquities.”

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">3</sty> Can two walk together, except they be agreed?

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">4</sty> Will a lion roar in the forest, when he has no prey? Will a young lion roar in his den, when nothing is taken?

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">5</sty> Will a bird fall into a snare on the ground without a fowler? Will a fowler take up his snare from the ground before he catches?

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">6</sty> Shall a trumpet sound in a city, and the people tremble not? Shall there be an evil in a city, which Jehovah hath not done?

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">7</sty> For nothing will the Lord Jehovah do, Except he has revealed his secret to his servants the Prophets.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">8</sty> The lion hath roared, who would not fear? The Lord Jehovah hath spoken, who would not prophesy?

--- PAGE 264 ---

[CENTERED_H1] <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ADDENDA TO AMOS</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300207></sty>AMOS 2:7, <sty c="800000" i="1">Who pant,</sty> etc. Our common version is the literal one,
[BODY] which is followed by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson,</sty> but altered for the worse by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome,</sty> who was led astray by Hobigant and the Septuagint. Not only are the words inverted, but a change is made in the verb, without the authority of any MS. It is a strong mode of expression to set forth the extreme cruelty and avariciousness of the persons referred to. “They even begrudged them,” as Henderson observes, “the small quantity of dust they had cast on their heads in token of mourning:” or, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty> says, They “<sty c="000000" i="1">longed</sty> to see the poor and miserable still more wretched: a most diabolical character surely!” To cast dust on the head was then customary in seasons of great distress. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><180212></sty>Job 2:12; <sty c="800000" i="0"><262730></sty>Ezekiel 27:30; and <sty c="800000" i="0"><101319></sty>2 Samuel 13:19.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300305></sty>AMOS 3:5, <sty c="800000" i="1">Will a bird fall into a snare on the earth without a</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">fowler</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">?</sty> etc. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome,</sty> with whom <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> materially agrees, renders this verse differently, —

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“Can a bird fall into a snare upon the earth,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Where no gin </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">is set </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">for him?</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Will a snare spring from the ground,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">When it hath not taken anything</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">?</sty><sty c="000080" i="1">”</sty>

[BODY] The comparison is more appropriate according to the version of Calvin; and the probability is that it is the right one. The word <sty c="008080" i="0">מוקש</sty>, rendered, “gin” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome,</sty> as it is in our version, is considered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> as redundant; and this very circumstance creates a suspicion that the passage is not rightly understood. This word is often rendered in our version “snare,” but it means more properly the act, ensnaring, than the instrument, snare: and it may be considered here a Hiphil participle — “he who ensnares,” or, “the ensnarer.” It is rendered <sty c="0000d4" i="0">iJxeuthv</sty>, a bird catcher by the Septuagint. Then the two following lines will admit naturally of Calvin’s rendering, and there will be no need of having recourse to <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson’s</sty> “elastic snare,” or to <sty c="000000" i="1">Adam Clarke’s</sty> “spring-trap,” neither of which is, I believe, intended by the word <sty c="008080" i="0">פח</sty>, which signifies a snare spread out, or an expanded net. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><181809></sty>Job 18:9; <sty c="800000" i="0"><19E005></sty>Psalm 140:5; 142:3.

--- PAGE 276 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300403></sty>AMOS 4:3, <sty c="800000" i="1">And ye shall cast yourselves down from the palace.</sty> This
[BODY] difficult line is variously rendered. <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty> renders it, “To Amenia shall ye be led away;” <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome,</sty> following <sty c="000000" i="1">Houbigant,</sty> who always mends the text, “And I will cast it forth, and will utterly destroy it;” <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson,</sty> “Ye shall be even thrown out of the palace;” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Horsley,</sty> following <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty>, “And ye shall be thrown into the shambles.” The whole difficulty lies in the word <sty c="008080" i="0">ההרמונה</sty>, found only here. The idea of shambles agrees well with the context, in which cows are mentioned. <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty>, after <sty c="000000" i="1">Schultens,</sty> derives it from <sty c="008080" i="0">הרם</sty>, not found in Hebrew as a verb, but it means in Arabic, “to cut into small pieces.” Hence as a noun, <sty c="008080" i="0">הרמון</sty> seems to denote the place in which meat is thus cut. The <sty c="008080" i="0">ה</sty>, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty> observes, is evidently radical, as it is preceded by <sty c="008080" i="0">ה</sty> servile. This appears to be the best solution of the difficulty.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300405></sty>AMOS 4:5, <sty c="800000" i="1">And burn incense</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> etc. The word for incense is not in the
[BODY] form of a verb. Both <sty c="000000" i="1">Houbigant</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Horsley</sty> take it here not a verb but a noun: but their transpositions are by no means necessary. The latter part of the fourth verse, and the whole of this, I would render thus, —

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And bring, in the morning, your sacrifices,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In the third year, your tithes,</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">5. And incense with the leaven of thank-offering,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yea, publish vows, proclaim </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">them;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For thus ye love </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">to do,</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> O children of Israel,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Saith the Lord Jehovah.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300524></sty>Amos 5:24, <sty c="800000" i="1">And run down</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> etc.,

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“But judgment shall come rolling on like waters,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And justice like a resistless torrent.</sty>

[BODY] “That is, the irresistible judgment and justice of God shall come upon those hypocrites like an inundation, and sweep them away like a torrent.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Horsley.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300601></sty>AMOS 6:1, <sty c="800000" i="1">which have been renowned</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> etc. Various are the
[BODY] expositions of this and the following line. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome’s </sty>version overleaps the rule of grammar, —

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“That are named after the chief of the nations,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And to them the house of Israel resort.”</sty>

--- PAGE 277 ---

[BODY] The explanation is this, — “They call themselves, not after their religious ancestors, but after the chief of the idolatrous nations, with whom they intermarry contrary to their law.” How foreign to everything in the context is this! <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson’s </sty>rendering is more like a version of the original, —

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“The distinguished men of the first of nations,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To whom the house of Israel come.”</sty>

[BODY] He considers that the chief persons of the Hebrew nation are here spoken of, to whom the house of Israel, the community, came for judgment: and this has little to do with the context. <sty c="000000" i="1">Horsley</sty> seems to have given the most literal version, and the most suitable to this place, —

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“Marking out the first of the nations,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Go unto them, O house of Israel.</sty>

[BODY] And then the Prophet mentions in the next who these nations were, “Pass over,” he says, “into Calneh,” etc. So that this distich is connected with the following verses. For <sty c="008080" i="0">נקבי</sty>, one copy of Kennicott has <sty c="008080" i="0">נקבו</sty>, “Mark ye out,” or, notice; this would make the line complete. Though the Septuagint here goes much astray, yet it gives the verb used here in the third person plural: and that person in Hebrew is the same as the second person plural of the imperative.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><300912></sty>AMOS 9:12, <sty c="800000" i="1">That possess they may,</sty> etc. Both this and the preceding
[BODY] verse are quoted, in <sty c="800000" i="0"><441516></sty>Acts 15:16, 17, not literally, but mainly from the Septuagint, which differs considerably in words, but not incense, from the Hebrew. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome, </sty>with the view of producing a verbal agreement, has changed and altered the Hebrew text without the authority of a single MS. This is rightly disapproved both by <sty c="000000" i="1">Adam Clarke</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson. </sty>The former says, “We must dismiss all these conjectural emendations, and take the Hebrew text as we find it. That it speaks of the <sty c="000000" i="1">conversion of the Jews</sty> in gospel times, we have the authority of the New Testament; and if we cannot make the <sty c="000000" i="1">words</sty>, as they stand there, <sty c="000000" i="1">entirely</sty> to agree with the words here, the <sty c="000000" i="1">subject</sty> is not affected by it.”

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 1</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT1</sty> There is an incongruity in our language in saying, “The words of Amos, which he <sty c="000000" i="1">saw</sty>.” To see words, except when written, is no proper expression. To avoid this, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> has paraphrased the passage thus,— “Which had come to him in a vision.” There would be no necessity for this, had we a suitable term for “words,” which in Hebrew has the same latitude of meaning with <sty c="0000d4" i="0">logov</sty> in Greek. Dathius renders it, <sty c="000000" i="1">Effata,</sty> oracles. They were the things, the matters, the events, which the Prophet saw, or were discovered to him in a supernatural manner. The faculty of sight seems to have been used, because scenes were presented often to the prophets, when these communications were made to them; and then seeing became the term to designate these divine revelations, when nothing but messages, either of mercy or of judgment, were conveyed to the prophets.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT2</sty> Rendered literally from the Hebrew, this verse is a fine specimen of sublime simplicity: the poetical inversion of words is preserved:— And he said,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Jehovah from Zion will roar,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And from Jerusalem will he send forth his voice;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Then mourn with the inhabitants of shepherds,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And wither will the top of Carmel.</sty>

[BODY] The roarings of lions are dreadful to shepherds. God’s voice is either of mercy or of judgment; it is the latter here, and evidently that of drought, (see <sty c="800000" i="0"><300506></sty>Amos 5:6,) as the withering of Carmel was to be the effect.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT3</sty> Eam non restituam—‘I will not restore it.’—<sty c="000000" i="1">Bishop Lowth</sty>. Of all commentators, Dathius gives the best explanation of the first part of this verse. His remarks are these:—“There is here mentioned a <sty c="000000" i="1">fourth</sty> sin, for which God would no longer defer punishment. The <sty c="000000" i="1">three</sty> sins, which had preceded the fourth, signify all those sins which they had besides committed, a definite number being put for a number

--- PAGE 279 ---

[BODY] indefinite.” But as to the phrase, <sty c="008080" i="0">לא אשיבנו</sty>, non avertam illud—‘I will not turn it away,’ so as to forgive it, that is, the fourth sin, he seems not to have been so felicitous; for the reference is evidently to Damascus. It will admit of either these renderings, —“I will not restore it,” that is, to favor; or “I will not turn away from it,” so as to let it go unpunished. The whole verse I would render thus:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Thus saith Jehova,—</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For three transgressions of Damascus,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yea, for the fourth, I will not turn away from it;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For it threshed Gelead with iron wains.</sty>

[BODY] Literally, it is, “<sty c="000000" i="1">they</sty> threshed;” for it is usual with the prophets, when speaking of a city or people, to pass from the singular to the plural number.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT4</sty> There were two Ben-hadads: the one whom Hazael strangled, <sty c="800000" i="0"><120815></sty>2 Kings 8:15; and his son who succeeded him, <sty c="800000" i="0"><121303></sty>2 Kings 13:3. But ben-hadad seems to have been the name of many of the kings of Syria, as Pharaoh was the common name of the kings of Egypt. Hence <sty c="000000" i="1">the</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">palaces of Ben-ha-dad</sty> were probably those built by several kings of that name.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 2</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT5</sty> This is one of the peculiarities of the Hebrew language—the use of two pronouns, the one before, and the other after the verb; and the preposition, when necessary, is given with the latter, and not the former. There is a similar peculiarity in the Welsh, exactly the same and of common occurrence. There are several instances of likeness between the two languages, and even an identity of idiom, and that in those things in which they differ from other languages. In the present instance the Welsh is literally, word for word, the Hebrew—<sty c="000000" i="1">y shai yr</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">aeth eich tadau ar eu hol—</sty> which your fathers have gone after them.— <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT6</sty> This verse has caused great labor to commentators; and many have been the views given. The first difficulty is in the words rendered in our version, “under you.” <sty c="008080" i="0">תחת</sty> and with the <sty c="000000" i="1">Iod</sty> commonly added when there is a suffix, often occurs, and means on doubt, an place, a spot, a standing, as in the following passages: <sty c="800000" i="0"><021023></sty>Exodus 10:23; 16:29;

--- PAGE 280 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><091409></sty>1 Samuel 14:9; <sty c="800000" i="0"><350316></sty>Habakkuk 3:16; and this seems to be its
[BODY] meaning here. Then the second difficulty is about “the cart” or wagon. Some consider it to be the vehicle to carry corn; and others, the machine to thresh it as Newcome and others do: but this view is not consistent with the other expressions used in this clause.
[BODY] A critic, quoted by Poole, evidently gives the meaning in these words, Sensus est, q.d. Ego vos in eas angustas adducam, unde vos ipsos mimime expedire valeatis — “The sense is, as though he said, I will bring you to those straits, from which ye will by no means be able to deliver yourselves.” I would then translate the verse thus:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Behold, I will confine </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">you</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> in your place,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">As a wagon confines its load—the sheaves;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">or word for word,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">As a wagon confines the filling of it—the sheaf.</sty>

[CENTERED] The rendering of the last line by Newcome is certainly not what the original will bear; his translation of the whole verse is this:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="0">Therefore</sty><sty c="000080" i="1">, behold I will press your place,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">As a loaded corn-wain presseth its sheaves.</sty>

[BODY] It is not pressing or crushing that corresponds with the contents of the following verses, but confining and reducing to straits from which they could not escape.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 3</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT7</sty> This is a mistake: Samaria was situated on a hill, and not on a plain: but there were hills or mountains surrounding it; so that what is said here equally applies to the place.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 4</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT8</sty> This word is commonly rendered ‘holiness,’ though it is also used to denote ‘the sanctuary.’ Calvin has been blamed for taking it here in the latter sense. What induced him to do so is evident from his comment: and when we consider all the circumstances of the passage, we may perhaps be disposed to think him right.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 281 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT9</sty> It is once applied in <sty c="800000" i="0"><202513></sty>Proverbs 25:13, to denote the piercing cold of snow; but its ideal meaning seems to be, pointed, piercing, penetrating: hence it means a thorn, a goad, and also a fishing-hook.— <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT10</sty> Literally, “on the third of days,” <sty c="008080" i="0">לשלשת ימים</sty>: but days here are evidently for years. “I cannot doubt,” says Dr. Henderson, “but that the Prophet has in view the enactment recorded in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><051429></sty>Deuteronomy 14:29, 26:12. <sty c="008080" i="0">ימים</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">days</sty>, mean here, as in <sty c="800000" i="0"><032529></sty>Leviticus 25:29, Judges 27:10, the fullest complement of days,
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e., a year.</sty>”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT11</sty> The words are, <sty c="008080" i="0">הרבות גנותיכם</sty>, “the abundance of your gardens,” and not your great or large gardens. I would thus render the verse—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">I smote you with blight and with mildew;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The abundance of your gardens and of your vineyards,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And your figs and your olives, the locust devoured;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">yet ye turned not to me, saith Jehovah.—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT12</sty> There seems to be a reference in “tuus” to the judgment denounced on Israel in the 2nd and 3rd verses of this chapter: he declares that he will deal with Israel “thus,” or in the manner before described.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 5</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT13</sty> This conjecture is fully borne out by the fact, that the copulative <sty c="008080" i="0">ו</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">vau</sty>, is found in more than twenty MSS., as given by Kennicott: it is also found in the Septuagint.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT14</sty> The verse, as evidently understood by Calvin, is to be thus rendered—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“He who made the Pleiades and Orion,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Who turns darkness into morning,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And darkens the day to night,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Who calls the waters of the sea,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And pours them on the face of the earth,—</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Jehovah is his name.”</sty>

[BODY] This is the rendering of the Septuagint. It is not consonant with the character of Hebrew to borrow a word, as it is done in our version, from a preceding verse. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> has prefixed the words, “That have forsaken;” on what authority it does not appear. The obvious construction of the passage given above.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 282 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT15</sty> Commentators are not agreed as to the meaning of the words rendered here, Pleiades and Orion, <sty c="008080" i="0">כימה</sty>, and <sty c="008080" i="0">כסיל</sty>. They are found only in two other places, <sty c="800000" i="0"><180909></sty>Job 9:9, and <sty c="800000" i="0"><183831></sty>Job 38:31; and in the first of these in conjunction with <sty c="008080" i="0">עש</sty>, in our version, Arcturus, and also in the second with <sty c="008080" i="0">מזרות</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Mazzaroth.</sty> Most think that all these were names given by the Hebrews to certain stars or constellations. It is evident, that with the exception of the last, Mazzaroth, the words, Pleiades, etcl, are names borrowed from the Greek poets, and first introduced by the translators of the Septuagint: but they observe no consistency; for in <sty c="800000" i="0"><180909></sty>Job 9:9, they render <sty c="008080" i="0">כימה</sty>, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">Arktourov</sty>, and in <sty c="800000" i="0"><183831></sty>Job 38:31; <sty c="0000d4" i="0">pleiav</sty> and in Amos the sentence is paraphrased and the word is left out. Again, <sty c="008080" i="0">כסיל</sty> is rendered <sty c="0000d4" i="0">e[sperov, </sty>the evening star, in <sty c="800000" i="0"><180909></sty>Job 9:9 and <sty c="0000d4" i="0">Wri>wnov</sty> in <sty c="800000" i="0"><183831></sty>Job 38:31 while <sty c="008080" i="0">עש</sty> is translated <sty c="0000d4" i="0">e[sperov</sty>, and in <sty c="800000" i="0"><180909></sty>Job 9:9, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">pleiav</sty>. This confusion proves that the translators exercised no discrimination. The Vulgate exhibits a similar inconsistency.
[BODY] Parkhurst’s view is the most satisfactory, and corresponds with the terms used in connection with the words in <sty c="800000" i="0"><183831></sty>Job 38:31, and with the context here. <sty c="000000" i="1">The genial heat, </sty>according to him, is <sty c="008080" i="0">כימה</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">the</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">cold </sty>is <sty c="008080" i="0">כסיל</sty>. The passage in Job is, “Canst thou bind the sweet influences of Pleuiades, <sty c="008080" i="0">כימה</sty>, or loose the bands of Orion? <sty c="008080" i="0">כסיל</sty>;” which influences <sty c="008080" i="0">מעדנות</sty>, the delights, the pleasures, the delicacies) of the genial heat, <sty c="008080" i="0">hmyk, </sty>or open contradictions of the cold? <sty c="008080" i="0">כסיל</sty> In the present passage what things God is continually doing are referred to, and not his past works, which would be the case where the constellations intended. Then the first line would run this:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">He who makes the genial heat and the cold.</sty>

[CENTERED] Thus the whole passage would agree well together, as relating the various acts of God as the supreme agent in the material world.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT16</sty> This version differs widely from ours, but is no doubt nearer the meaning of the original. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome’s</sty> translation is as follows—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Who scattereth desolation over the strong,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And bringeth desolation over the fortress.</sty>

[BODY] What comes nearest the original is the rendering proposed by Parkhurst, and is striking and poetical: the first line is this—

--- PAGE 283 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Who causeth the waster to laugh at strength.</sty>

[BODY] The verb here used means primarily to laugh or to smile, and in a secondary sense, to be refreshed or made cheerful, or to be comforted. It is here in Hiphil, the causative mood. The whole verse may be thus rendered:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Who causeth the waster to laugh at strength;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And a waster on the fortress he will bring.—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT17</sty> The verb is <sty c="008080" i="0">בושסכם</sty>, from <sty c="008080" i="0">בושם</sty> but in ten MSS. Of Kennicott, and in five of D’Rossi, the <sty c="008080" i="0">ו</sty> is left out: and then <sty c="008080" i="0">ש</sty> is supposed to be put for <sty c="008080" i="0">ס</sty>, as Amos in another place, <sty c="800000" i="0"><300714></sty>Amos 7:14, puts <sty c="008080" i="0">ס</sty> for <sty c="008080" i="0">ש</sty>. The verb <sty c="008080" i="0">בס</sty>, and in its reduplicate form <sty c="008080" i="0">בסס</sty>, occurs in other places, and means to tread or trample under foot. The expression here literally is, “your trampling;” but such a form may often be expressed in our language, “ye trample.” The connection of the whole verse will be better seen by the following version:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Therefore, as ye trample on the poor,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And tribute of corn extort from him,—</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Houses of hewn stone you may build,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">But ye shall not dwell in them;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Vineyards of delight ye may plant,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">But ye shall not drink their wine.—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT18</sty> This verse literally rendered from the Hebrew is as follows:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Therefore, the prudent at that time will be silent;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For a time of evil </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">will</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> that </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">be.</sty>

[BODY] It is a rule, which may be viewed as nearly if not wholly, universal, that the substantive verb, <sty c="000000" i="1">to be</sty>, whenever understood and not expressed in the original, is to be rendered in the same tense with the other verbs in the sentence. So here, “A time of evil <sty c="000000" i="1">will</sty> that <sty c="000000" i="1">be</sty>,” and not, “<sty c="000000" i="1">is</sty>;” for the previous verb is in the future tense.
[BODY] There are many instances of the neglect of this rule in our version, as in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><192304></sty>Psalm 23:4, “I will fear no evil: for thou <sty c="000000" i="1">art</sty> with me;” it ought
[BODY] to be, “for thou <sty c="000000" i="1">wilt</sty> be with me.” And then the line which follows ought be rendered in the same way, for the verb is in the future tense, —“Thy rod and thy staff, they will comfort me.”<sty c="000000" i="1">—Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT19</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> gives a better rendering of these two lines,—

--- PAGE 284 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">In all the broad places </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">there shall be</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> wailing,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And in all the streets, they shall say, Oh! Oh!</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="008080" i="0">רחבות</sty>, from <sty c="008080" i="0">רחב</sty>, to be dilated, and to be made broad or wide, mean broad places or broad streets: and <sty c="008080" i="0">חוצות</sty>, from <sty c="008080" i="0">חצה</sty>, to divide, signify the common streets, by which the town is divided. The exclamations, <sty c="008080" i="0">הו</sty>, <sty c="008080" i="0">הו</sty>, are rendered by Calvin, Vae! Vae! <sty c="000000" i="1">Eheu,</sty> in Latin, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Woe,</sty> in our language come nearest to sound in the original.<sty c="000000" i="1">—Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT20</sty> There appears here a great candor in our Author: but the first view of the passage seems the most natural and obvious, as presented in our version, with which that of <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> agrees. Having before exhorted them to “take away” what they thought much of, the Prophet now exhorts them to attend to judgment and justice. The two verses, 23 and 24, may be thus rendered:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">23. Remove from me the multitude of thy songs,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the music of thy harps; I will not hear </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">them.</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">24.And let judgment roll down like waters,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And righteousness like a mighty stream.</sty>

[BODY] I prefer rendering <sty c="008080" i="0">המון</sty>, “multitude,” with Calvin, rather than “noise,” with our version and <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome,</sty> or “sound” with <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. It forms a variety as to the next clause. In idiomatic English the expressions would be—“thy many songs and thy harmonious harps.” The two verses ought to be read as connected; and the 24th should begin with “And,” <sty c="008080" i="0">ו</sty>, and not “But.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT21</sty> No commentator has given us a satisfactory rendering of these two verses. Perhaps that of Calvin, as a whole, comes nearest to the original. The question, <sty c="000000" i="1">Have ye,</sty> etc., is considered by many as not implying a negation but a concession, as though it had been said, “I grant this; ye did offer,” etc.; and then, what is said in verse 26 was what they did besides. It was this mixture of two worships, the worship of God and the worship of idols, that is here brought against the Israelites. I venture to present the following translation:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Did you bring me sacrifices and oblation in the wilderness</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For forty years, O house of Israel?</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">You did also bear Sacut, your king,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And Kiun,</sty><sty c="000080" i="0"> which were </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">your images;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">A star </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">was</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> your god,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Which ye formed for yourselves.</sty>

--- PAGE 285 ---

[BODY] That the hosts of heaven were the objects of their worship, is evident from Stephen’s Sermon in <sty c="800000" i="0"><440742></sty>Acts 7:42, “Then God turned and gave them up to worship the host of heaven.” Stephen then refers to, and quotes this passage, not from the Hebrew, but almost literally from the Septuagint. Instead of, “their figures which ye have made for yourselves,” he has, “figures which ye made to worship them.” He gives the meaning, but not the words.
[BODY] Between the words of Amos, in Hebrew, and those of Stephen, there is a material, though not verbal agreement. Two objects of idolatrous worship are mentioned, and also their images, but their names are different. The probability is, that those used by Amos were not current at the time the Greek version was made, and that the names by which those deities were then known were used. Moloch, indeed, means a king, but applied, like Baal, to several heathen gods; and Kiun is said to be Arabic, and Remphan is an Egyptic term, designating the same star or planet, which critics suppose to have been Saturn. Moloch, as Grotius suggests, had the figure of a king, and Kiun that of a star.
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT22</sty> Here is another instance in which the meaning and not the words, is given by Stephen in <sty c="800000" i="0"><440743></sty>Acts 7:43. In this instance, the Septuagint is the same with the Hebrew text, “beyond Damascus;” but Stephen says, “beyond Babylon.” The same quarter is meant, though the name of the place is different.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT23</sty> There seems to be a peculiar propriety in introducing this sentence. The Israelites became the worshippers of the hosts of heaven; then the Prophet says, that Jehovah is the <sty c="000000" i="1">God of Hosts</sty>. What folly, then, it was to worship the hosts of heaven, and to forsake the God of them!—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 6</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT24</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="800000" i="1"> </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">“Woe to them that are at ease in Zion,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And to them that are secure in the mountain of Samaria.”</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Dr. Henderson.</sty>

--- PAGE 286 ---

[BODY] From not considering the main drift of what follows in this chapter, critics have proposed emendations in this verse. The careless and the secure, both at Jerusalem and Samaria, are evidently meant. Newcome renders the last line nearly the same with Henderson—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“And that rest secure in the mountain of Samaria.”</sty>

[BODY] So that the word “trust” in our version is not correct. The word used means often to be confident or secure, as well as to trust; but the law of parallelism requires it to be in the former sense here; as they were at ease in Zion, so they were confident or secure on the mount of Samaria.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT25</sty> The words are <sty c="008080" i="0">וסר מרזה סרוחים</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">usar merezach saruchim,</sty> —an instance of striking alliteration. But Calvin’s rendering, though amounting in its general import to the same thing, is certainly not the correct one. <sty c="008080" i="0">סר</sty> never means to come, but the reverse, to depart. To decline, to turn aside or away, or to depart, is its common signification. Then <sty c="008080" i="0">מרזה</sty> is properly shouting, either for grief or for joy; here evidently for the latter; and it may be rendered here mirth; so the clause may be thus translated—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And depart shall the mirth of the recumbents,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">or</sty><sty c="000080" i="1">, of those who stretch themselves.</sty>

[INDENT] Dr. Henderson’s version is the following:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And the shouting company of those that recline shall depart.</sty>

[BODY] The translation of Symmachus is, “Taken away shall be the company of the voluptuous, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">eJtairei>a trufhtwn</sty>” The idea of “banquet” for the word here used, is what the Rabbins have given to it.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT26</sty> This is the view taken by Matthew Henry, and seems not unsuitable. “The methods used for their reformation,” he says, “have been all fruitless and ineffectual. <sty c="000000" i="1">Shall horses run</sty>, etc. No; for there will be no profit to countervail the pains. God has sent them his prophets to break up their fallow ground; but they found them as hard and inflexible as the rock, rough and rugged, and they could do no good with them, nor work upon them, and therefore they shall not attempt it any more.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT27 </sty><sty c="000000" i="1">“Ye have turned judgment into gall,</sty> which is nauseous, <sty c="000000" i="1">and the fruit of</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">righteousness into hemlock,</sty> which is noxious.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">M. Henry.</sty>

--- PAGE 287 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT28</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Jurisdicto</sty>, which means sometimes the authority to determine the import of the law; but it means here the power to interpret the thoughts of the heart.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 7</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT29</sty> The verb is <sty c="008080" i="0">הראני</sty>, “made” or caused “me to see;” and so to render it would have been better; and it is the same verb in the succeeding clauses.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT30</sty> One MS. has <sty c="008080" i="0">יקים</sty>, which is, to cause to rise, or to raise up. This agrees with the Septuagint—<sty c="0000d4" i="0">anasth>sei</sty>, and comports with the rest of the sentence; for <sty c="008080" i="0">מי</sty> is ‘who,’ and not ‘how’ or ‘by what means.’ — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT31</sty> The Masoretic point, called <sty c="000000" i="1">Holem</sty>, is referred to, which, being put above the <sty c="008080" i="0">ו</sty> after the first radical letter, or in absence of the <sty c="008080" i="0">ו</sty>, denotes a participle.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT34</sty> The fabled queen of hell.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT36</sty> So it is literally <sty c="008080" i="0">בית ממלכה</sty>. Newcome renders it, “The temple of the kingdom.” Henderson, “The royal residence.” Grotius, <sty c="000000" i="1">Sedes</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">imperii,</sty> “The seat of the empire.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 8</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT38</sty> The literal rendering of the verse seems to be this—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And they shall howl the songs of the temple:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Many a dead body </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">shall be </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">in every place;—</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">“Cast </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">it</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> away, be silent.”</sty>

[BODY] The expressions are abrupt, but very striking. What would be commonly said is mentioned, “Cast it away,” etc. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> translates as follows:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">There shall be </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">many dead bodies in every place:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">And men shall say</sty><sty c="000080" i="1">, Cast forth, be silent.”</sty>

--- PAGE 288 ---

[CENTERED] Very tame is this, compared with the original literally rendered. To introduce, <sty c="000000" i="1">And men shall say,</sty> lessens the force of the sentence.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT39</sty> Shake or move is the most current meaning of the word, and the most suitable to this place. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it, “be shaken;”<sty c="000000" i="1"> Henderson,</sty> “tremble;” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius,</sty> “be moved.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT40</sty> A different view is given by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and also by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson.</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> translates thus,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">shall not</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> all of it rise up as the river,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And be driven out </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">of its place</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> and sunk down as the river of Egypt?</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> renders the lines in the same sense, though in different words,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall not all of it rise like the river?</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall it not be driven and subside</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Like the river of Egypt?</sty>

[BODY] The question is unnecessarily retained, borrowed from the first line of the verse. It is seldom, if ever, that this is the case in Hebrew; it is not consistent with the simplicity of the language. It is evidently the earthquake that is here compared to the rising and subsiding of a river. I would therefore render the whole verse thus,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall not for this the land shake,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And every inhabitant in it mourn?</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For heave up as a river shall the whole of it,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And it shall be agitated and subside like the river of Egypt.</sty>

[BODY] Here is the heaving, the agitation, and the subsidence of the earth in an earthquake.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT41</sty> He means evidently the <sty c="000000" i="1">Keri</sty>, the marginal reading.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT42</sty> Both this and the former line are rendered differently by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, more consistently with the words of the original—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And I will make it as a mourning for an only </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">son,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the end thereof as a day of bitterness.</sty>

[BODY] The pronoun “it,” and also “thereof,” is the feminine <sty c="008080" i="0">ה</sty>: <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> refers it to <sty c="008080" i="0">הדברה</sty>, this matter, or this event, understood: or in case <sty c="008080" i="0">ארץ</sty>, land, be the antecedent, he thinks that <sty c="008080" i="0">כאבל</sty>, “as a mourning,” should be rendered participially, as “one who mourns.” Either of these constructions may suit the original; but another seems preferable. The

--- PAGE 289 ---

[BODY] antecedent to “it appears to be <sty c="008080" i="0">אבל</sty>, “mourning,” in the first line of the verse. Our own version is no doubt the correct one, and not that which Calvin adopts; only the last line may be better rendered thus, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius</sty> do—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“And the end of it as that of the bitterest day.”—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 9</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT43</sty> Calvin is not without many expounders, who agree with him in this view; yet the reasons assigned do not apply. “Though the true God,” as <sty c="000000" i="1">Dr. Henderson</sty> justly observes, “was seen beside the idolatrous altar, it was not for the purpose of receiving homage, but of commanding that the whole of the erection and worship at Bethel should be destroyed.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT44</sty> These two lines are variously explained. The words can hardly admit the meaning here given to them. The scene was in the temple, and worshippers were present. The command was to strike the lintel; the fall of the pillars or posts was the consequence: many were destroyed, and those who remained were to be killed by the sword, and not one was to escape. There seems to be here an allusion to two previous events—the shaking and pulling down of the pillars of the house of Dagon by Sampson,—and the slaughter of the priests of Baal by Jehu. I render the verse thus:— I saw the Lord standing on the altar, and he said,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">“Strike the lintel, that the pillars may shake,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And break them down on the head of them all;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the remainder of them with the sword will I slay;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Flee away from them shall not he who fleeth,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And escape from them shall not he who escapeth.”</sty>

[CENTERED] <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius,</sty> as well as <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, render the third and fourth lines, where the difficulty alone exists, according to the version given above; and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> renders the third line materially the same,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And break them in pieces on the heads of them all.</sty>

[BODY] But he retains “posterity” in the fourth line, which seems not consistent with the tenor of the passage.
[INDENT] The version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius</sty> is this,—

--- PAGE 290 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Et divide ipsos in capite ipsorum omnium,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Quod autem post ipsos est gladio interrficam.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty> is more paraphrastic, and gives the same sense,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Eosque diffinde </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">ut ruant</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> in caput omnium</sty><sty c="000080" i="0"> qui adsunt,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Reliquos vero gladio interficam.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome, </sty>who is too fond of emendations, folllows <sty c="000000" i="1">Houbigant,</sty> who, for no reason that appears, turns the verb into the first person; and he gives this rendering of the third line,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">For I will wound them in the head, </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">even</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> all of them:</sty>

[BODY] But this evidently does not comport with the context.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT45</sty> This verse and the preceding are connected together, and form but one period, as it evidently appears from the construction of the words in Hebrew. The following may be considered a literal version:—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">5. And the Lord, Jehovah of hosts,—</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Who touches the earth and it melts,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And mourn do all who dwell on it,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And it heaves up as a river, the whole of it,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And sinks down as the river of Egypt,—</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">6. Who builds in the heavens his ascents,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And fixes on the earth his station,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Who calls the waters of the sea,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And pours them forth on the face of the earth,—</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Jehovah is his name.—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT46</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Caenacula,</sty> in other places, <sty c="000000" i="1">ascensiones,</sty> and more correctly; for
[CENTERED] <sty c="008080" i="0">מעלות</sty> is properly ascents, steps to ascend, stairs; and hence, the places ascended to, chambers.
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT47</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Coagmentationes suas—</sty>his joinings, cementings; but <sty c="008080" i="0">אנדתו</sty> is in the singular number. It is difficult to ascertain its meaning. It occurs not as a verb, but from its application, its ideal meaning seems to be, to join or bind together, so as to form a compact body. It is applied in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><100225></sty>2 Samuel 2:25, to designate a <sty c="000000" i="1">troop</sty>, a compact body of men. It
[BODY] signifies in <sty c="800000" i="0"><020722></sty>Exodus 7:22, a <sty c="000000" i="1">bundle</sty> of hyssop. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it a <sty c="000000" i="1">storehouse,</sty> deriving its meaning from the Chaldee; and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> renders it <sty c="000000" i="1">vault,</sty> tracing it from the Arabic, and says that it signifies the vault or arch of heaven, the hemispheric expanse, which apparently to

--- PAGE 291 ---

[BODY] the eye is founded on the earth; but a <sty c="000000" i="1">band </sty>or <sty c="000000" i="1">troop</sty> has been its most common acceptation.
[BODY] It must be borne in mind, that it must be something on earth that corresponds or forms a contrast with <sty c="000000" i="1">ascents </sty>in the heavens. God has his ascendings, or as it were, his steps or stairs in the heavens, along which, speaking after the manner of men, he ascends: then what has he on earth? It seems to me that something firm, solid, compacted, is intended; and the earth is said to be his footstool. Hence a firm footing, standing, or station, appears to be the meaning of the word. The French translation is—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Qui fonde son batiment sur la terre—</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">“Who founds his </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">building</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> on the earth.”—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT48 </sty><sty c="008080" i="0">עשה זאת</sty>, “who <sty c="000000" i="1">will</sty> do these things.” It appears that <sty c="008080" i="0">זאת</sty> is a pronoun which is plural as well as singular: and the Welsh <sty c="000000" i="1">hyn</sty> is exactly the same; it will admit thing or things to be added to it—<sty c="000000" i="1">y peth</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">hyn—y pethan hyn.</sty> When it is put by itself, <sty c="000000" i="1">hyn</sty> it conveys the idea of one particular thing, or of several things, according to the context.
[BODY] The relative pronoun, <sty c="008080" i="0">אשר</sty>, who, whom, is of the same character; it is both singular and plural, as <sty c="000000" i="1">whom </sty>also is in the English language, and <sty c="000000" i="1">sawl</sty> in Welsh.
[BODY] <sty c="008080" i="0">עשה</sty> is considered a participle, and the participle in Hebrew may often be rendered as a personal noun, and in this instance <sty c="000000" i="1">the doer.</sty> It is the <sty c="0000d4" i="0">oJ</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">poiwn</sty> of the Greek. But if the verbal form be retained, the auxiliary verb must be in the same tense with the leading verb in the context, “who <sty c="000000" i="1">will</sty> do these things.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT49</sty> This sentence is an instance, common in Hebrew, of the use of two pronouns, —a relative and a personal pronoun; to the latter of which is prefixed the preposition. It has already been noticed, that in Welsh the same idiom exists; in that language this line is rendered word for word like the Hebrew: and the true rendering is no doubt that which is mentioned last by Calvin. The Hebrew literally is this,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Whom shall be called my name upon them.</sty>

[CENTERED] The same line in Welsh, without any change even in the order of the words,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Y rhai y gelwir vy enw arnyt.</sty>

--- PAGE 292 ---

[BODY] Another peculiarity is, that the preposition is prefixed and <sty c="000000" i="1">joined to</sty> the personal pronoun in Welsh as well as in Hebrew; and a third is, that the relative <sty c="000000" i="1">y rhai</sty> (the whom) in Welsh, like <sty c="008080" i="0">אשר</sty> in Hebrew, admits of no case. It is the same when a nominative to a verb, or when an accusative governed by it.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT50</sty> There is no Prayer here, for the Lecture is not completed: it includes a
[INDENT] portion of the Book of Obadiah.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">TRANSLATION OF AMOS FOOTNOTE</sty>

[CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="0">FT51 </sty>“And travel not to Beersheba,” or “make no pilgrimage to Beersheba.”
[CENTERED] The verb <sty c="008080" i="0">עבר</sty> expresses going abroad to another country. Beersheba belonged to the kingdom of Judah.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Bp. Horsley.</sty>
